AI, Infrastructure, and the Next Investment Cycle

a16z(298.0K)·52:21·7.9K views·2026-09-30
David GeorgeGeneral Partner, a16z Sarah WangGeneral Partner, a16z Alex ImmermanGeneral Partner, a16z
TL;DRDriven heavily by tech (which accounts for ~55% of US capital spending and nearly 40% of aggregate stock market value), current market highs are supported by fundamental earnings rather than multiple expansion.
Meanwhile, AI infrastructure buildout and agentic workflows are...

TL;DR

Driven heavily by tech (which accounts for ~55% of US capital spending and nearly 40% of aggregate stock market value), current market highs are supported by fundamental earnings rather than multiple expansion. Meanwhile, AI infrastructure buildout and agentic workflows are accelerating demand across compute, power, and physical supply chains.

Macro, Markets, and the Infrastructure Boom

  • High-Tech Capital Concentration:
    • High-tech equipment, software, and R&D account for roughly 55% of US capital spending (David George).
    • US tech companies represent 8 of the top 10 valued companies globally, with tech comprising almost 40% of the aggregate US stock market value (David George).
    • Model companies combined have raised over $350 billion (David George).
  • Earnings-Driven Market Highs:
    • The market is up 90% (17% annualized) since JBT came out almost 4 years ago (Alex Immerman, David George).
    • Stocks are up ~20% while trading multiples are down ~20%, meaning performance is driven by fundamental earnings rather than multiple expansion (David George).
    • The S&P 500 earnings multiple is below 20x, while cyclical memory companies trade at 6x–7x forward earnings (David George).
  • Massive Hyperscaler CapEx & Infrastructure Scale:
    • The AI infrastructure buildout has surpassed railroads as a percentage of GDP (David George).
    • CapEx for the five largest hyperscalers (Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, Oracle) is projected at $416 billion in 2025, $780 billion in 2026, and over $1 trillion annually from 2027 onward (David George).
    • Combined cloud backlog for Microsoft, Google, and Amazon sits at roughly $1.7 trillion (David George).
  • The "Age of Atoms" & Power Grids:
    • Global infrastructure investment needs are estimated at $90 trillion through 2040 across power, water, roads, transit, and manufacturing (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • Portfolio examples include Anduril’s manufacturing facility (spanning 87 football fields), Waymo depot expansions, and SpaceX's $100 billion investment in Louisiana (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • Data centers can actually help lower residential electricity rates; a US study showed that for every 10% increase in data center capacity, residential electricity rates decreased by 40 basis points due to spreading fixed grid costs over more units of electricity (Santiago Rodriguez, Alex Immerman).

Models, Applications, and Enterprise Adoption

  • Rapid Revenue Growth at the Model Layer:
    • Combined annualized revenue for OpenAI and Anthropic has climbed significantly, well-surpassing net new revenue estimates for the greatest software companies in history (Sarah Wang).
  • Early Enterprise Diffusion:
    • Live deployments at S&P 500 companies stand at 69%, but quantifiable impact sits at 30%, and tracked recurring impact is only at 2% (Sarah Wang).
    • Most enterprise exposure remains limited to Microsoft Copilot (Sarah Wang).
    • Forward-leaning large Fortune 500 companies allocate roughly 1% of headcount-equivalent spend to AI tools, whereas AI-native portfolio companies reach up to 10% (David George).
  • Power Users Pulling Away:
    • Median AI vendor spending in the top 1% of users is roughly 8x the level of the top 10% (Sarah Wang).
    • In AI-native portfolio companies, top users spend $7,500 to $9,000 per month, compared to median users spending $200 to $400 monthly (Sarah Wang).
  • Measurable ROI and Cost Reductions:
    • Chime reduced its cost-to-serve by over 10% annually for four years (compounding to nearly a 50% total reduction), supported by Decagon (Alex Immerman).
    • Shopify's AI sidekick helped increase the percentage of onboarded merchants reaching 5 orders within 15 days by 8% (Alex Immerman).
    • ServiceNow reported over $1 billion in AI annual contract value (ACV) and a 9x increase in agentic deployments (Sarah Wang).
    • Stripe's SAS customer data shows growth accelerating into 2026 across both young and mature businesses, a dynamic Stripe calls "the renaissance" (Alex Immerman, Sarah Wang).

Agents, Costs, and Efficiency Gains

  • Plummeting Inference Costs & Agent Scaling:
    • Agent token usage on Open Router experienced 14x growth (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • Context caching has drastically lowered costs (e.g., Hebia saw financial chat workloads become 10x cheaper to run) (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • Advanced routing (e.g., Data Bricks' smart router) solves more problems at 35% lower cost than the strongest individual model (Sarah Wang).
    • Fine-tuning smaller models (e.g., Elise AI fine-tuning a model to be 60% cheaper with lower latency) makes live audio use cases tenable (Sarah Wang).

Consumer AI and Marketplaces

  • Consumer Adoption and Engagement:
    • Just over 2% of US households have a paying subscription for AI (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • AI subscriptions exhibit "smiling retention curves" that flatline and actually rise as products improve (Santiago Rodriguez, Alex Immerman).
  • Marketplace Impact and Frictionless Commerce:
    • While Amazon declined integration with Muse, Instacart integrated it, looking to capture incremental orders and GMV from automated agentic purchasing (Santiago Rodriguez).
    • Search engines like Google have remained resilient because high-monetizing ad terms (e.g., buying insurance, booking hotels) involve intent that AI has not fully automated on the user's behalf (Alex Immerman).

Public and Private Software Markets

  • Public Software Biffurcation:
    • Roughly 75% of public software companies are profitable, but only 30% are growing at 20% or more (Sarah Wang).
    • Cybersecurity (e.g., CrowdStrike), observability, and vertical software have held up significantly better than horizontal applications (Sarah Wang).
  • Late-Stage Private Champions:
    • The top 6 private companies (Anthropic, OpenAI, Databricks, Stripe, Waymo, Revolut) have a combined last-round valuation of $2.4 trillion—larger than the combined market cap of all IPOs over the last 10 years excluding SpaceX ($1.7 trillion) (Alex Immerman).
  • Secondary Liquidity Dynamics:
    • Employee participation in company-held tender offers tracked by Carta is only 58%, reflecting high employee conviction in business performance (Alex Immerman).
    • Discounts on secondary market transactions relative to last-round prices are essentially zero, contrasting with previous years (Alex Immerman).

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • CapEx Projections: Hyperscaler CapEx will exceed $1 trillion annually starting in 2027 — David George
  • Cumulative Tech Investment: Cumulative technology investment numbers will be 20x higher over the next 5 to 7, potentially 10 years — David George
  • Autonomous Driving Scale: Fully autonomous driving networks (currently 14x safer than human drivers) will expand miles traveled by at least an order of magnitude, and all 17 million new cars sold annually in the US will be autonomous over the next 10 years — David George
  • Robotics Investment: Robotics investment will become even larger than LLMs (though arriving 3 to 5 years earlier), driving massive investment and excitement over the next 5 years — David George
  • Military Hardware Diffusion: Newer defense vendors (like Anduril, Saronic, and Castellian) will grow dramatically from their current baseline of under 5% of overall military dollars spent as needs change — David George

Notable Quotes

"Tech is driving the investment cycle obviously across all areas of the economy... and just to put it into into very deep historical context... this buildout just surpassed railroads as a percentage of GDP." — David George
"AI is generating major revenue and savings. On the other hand, adoption is still extremely early." — David George
"The AI can know a lot about the world but know very little about your company. And we're seeing this in our application layer companies where they're bridging this gap." — Sarah Wang
"Investing in physical infrastructure is very different than investing in software. Elon coined the term 'the factory is the product' many years ago. It's no surprise that a lot of the great founders that we've backed have come out of Tesla or SpaceX." — Santiago Rodriguez

Citadel CEO Ken Griffin & Carnegie Mellon President Farnam Jahanian Talk Donation, New Campus |...

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·11:42·32 views·2026-09-30
Ken GriffinCEO, Citadel Farnam JahanianPresident, Carnegie Mellon University Lisa AbramowiczAnchor, Bloomberg
TL;DRCitadel CEO Ken Griffin is committing a record $3 billion to Carnegie Mellon University—with $2 billion funding a new campus in Miami and $1 billion supporting the main Pittsburgh campus, including naming the Kenneth C.
Griffin School of Computer Science—to pioneer a...

TL;DR

Citadel CEO Ken Griffin is committing a record $3 billion to Carnegie Mellon University—with $2 billion funding a new campus in Miami and $1 billion supporting the main Pittsburgh campus, including naming the Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science—to pioneer a tech-forward, interdisciplinary model for higher education focused on return on investment and grand societal challenges.

The $3 Billion Carnegie Mellon Donation & Strategy

  • Record-Breaking Financial Commitment:
    • Citadel founder Ken Griffin is making a $3 billion gift to Carnegie Mellon University (CMU), marking the largest single gift ever committed to a U.S. university and doubling his total philanthropic investments in CMU.
  • Allocation of Funds:
    • $2 billion is designated to build and establish a new campus in Miami.
    • $1 billion goes to the main campus in Pittsburgh, which includes naming the institution's top-ranked computer science program the Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science.
  • Institutional Rankings & ROI:
    • According to Ken Griffin, U.S. News rankings place CMU number three in the country for return on investment on a college education, propelling the school to number 14 overall in the U.S.

A New Model for Higher Education

  • Breaking Down Silos:
    • According to President Farnam Jahanian, the new Miami campus and educational model will organize around grand societal challenges (such as human health, national security, cyber security, and energy/climate resiliency) rather than traditional departments, colleges, and schools.
  • Balancing Technical Rigor and the Humanities:
    • Ken Griffin emphasizes the need for well-rounded students who combine foundational technical and scientific knowledge with human skills like problem-solving, communication, and ethical application.
    • President Jahanian and Griffin noted CMU's deep roots in the humanities, highlighting alumni achievements including nearly 70 Tony Award winners, the first school of drama in the U.S. (founded in 1914), and artist Andy Warhol.
  • Urban Re-industrialization and Economic Impact:
    • Pittsburgh’s economic renaissance is anchored by CMU, which currently features 100 different robotics companies driving the re-industrialization of the U.S.
    • Griffin intends for the new CMU Miami campus to similarly transform Miami by attracting hundreds of technology companies, startups, and career opportunities.

Higher Education Reform and Core Mission

  • Shifting the Campus Narrative:
    • Ken Griffin stated that American campuses have improved over the past two years (since 2024), noting that the pendulum is shifting back to the center after universities briefly "lost the narrative" of preparing students for the workforce.
  • The Ethos of College:
    • Griffin criticized viewing college as "four years of fun" or "four years of an entitlement," arguing that the true ethos should be preparing young people to make a tangible difference in America.
    • President Jahanian affirmed that universities must stay true to their core mission of educating the next generation, creating knowledge to benefit society, and driving economic and cultural impact.

Notable Quotes

"This is an investment in excellence. Carnegie Melon University leads the nation in computer science, in artificial intelligence, in cyber security. Worldrenowned program robotics. This is a university that represents the future of our country." — Ken Griffin
"Whenever there are massive societal and technological changes, CMU steps up. We make big bold bets. And this is one of those I think given the changes that we see in technology advances we see in AI there's no question that every sector of our economy is being transformed." — Farnam Jahanian
"The point of higher education is to educate our young men and women to lead our country, to run businesses, to create new products, to entertain us, to actually enter the workforce and have tangible skills in solving problems." — Ken Griffin

They'll solve our important problems, they will build big things, and they will do so with urgency. And if you look at the economic prospects of the students who graduate from CMU, they are outstanding. This is what a university should deliver. And I hope that this gift catalyzes discussions across the country, in the boards of trustees and in the C-suites of colleges and universities, to focus on what best practices we need to bring into our four walls that we see at a Carnegie Mellon, that we see at an MIT, and that we see at a Stanford that will drive the success of our students and the prosperity of our country. Let me build on that. You asked earlier what this new model for education is going to look like. We're not thinking in terms of departments, colleges, and schools. We're really thinking about grand challenges, big societal issues, and opportunities—as Ken likes to call them—like human health, national security, cybersecurity, innovation, energy, and climate resiliency. We are organizing the campus around those, bringing our learning and research to bear, not in the context of disciplinary silos, but bringing people together to address these grand challenges. This is really the vision for the future. When you ask about what we teach our students, we need to teach them foundations, there's no question. But we also need to teach them inherent human skills: critical thinking, problem-solving. These students are going to go out there and change the world. They need to be able to essentially not only have knowledge and skills that are technical and scientific, but also the human skills that are going to serve them well as we shape the future. And I think if you look out the window here at Pittsburgh, one of the great industrial success stories of America, the renaissance of Pittsburgh is being created here at Carnegie Mellon University. There are a hundred different robotics companies here in Pittsburgh, leading the way for the re-industrialization of the United States of America. That is being driven by the students, faculty, and resources of Carnegie Mellon University. So, this is your biggest gift ever, $3 billion. It's a massive sum. It breaks all records when it comes to a single gift to a single institution. You're focused also on building out the Miami branch, and you're talking about your efforts to try to create Miami as a world-class city with a pipeline of talent. Is this going to be your focus? Is there more to come with this, or is it really CMU that you're putting your focus on to try to see what kind of results you can get? I'd like to think that I have decades ahead of me to explore that question more fully. But this gift, the magnitude of this gift, represents the magnitude of the aspiration. I truly believe that just as Carnegie Mellon University is transforming Pittsburgh—a renaissance for the 21st century—that CMU Miami will transform Miami, bringing to Miami hundreds of technology companies, startups, career opportunities, and futures that impact both the people of Miami and the greater prosperity of our nation. I just wonder if this means that going forward, the new model of education needs to be something a little bit more technically driven with respect to skills that are applicable in the real world. Does the classic concept of the Ivy League, well-rounded human, still have a place for $500,000 or $400,000 in the world that we live in today? We need well-rounded humans more than ever before. Think about advances in technology. Think about the impact of that on society. We need to train engineers and scientists who understand the implications of those advances, the implication of that on society and on communities, and be able to navigate it. So in fact, I think not only do we need to give them technical knowledge and scientific knowledge, we also need to give them, as I mentioned, problem-solving skills, communication skills, and the ethical applications of the technologies that they develop. All of that goes into it, and this is in fact part of the reason why, when we talk about these grand societal challenge areas, we want to bring an interdisciplinary group of people to work together. This is really the wave of the future. I actually think that interdisciplinary point is really important. I had the benefit of a liberal arts degree and a strong background in computer science. It's been defining to my life. Carnegie Mellon has an incredible history in the humanities. There are almost 70 Tony Award winners among Carnegie Mellon alumni. It is the first school of drama in the United States, founded in 1914, and Andy Warhol went to school here. I know, and I've heard that the museum is pretty amazing. I do wonder, though, going forward: you had some pretty scathing comments about some of the universities a couple of years ago when you were talking about where you disagreed with their approach. Do you think things have gotten better since 2024? Things have improved over the course of the last two years across American campuses. Let's use the word "pointed," because we lost the narrative. The point of higher education is to educate our young men and women to lead our country, to run businesses, to create new products, to entertain us, and to actually enter the workforce with tangible skills in solving problems. We lost that narrative to a variety of other issues that just don't truly matter to the future prosperity and well-being of our country. Do you feel like it's coming back to some degree? There's no doubt the pendulum is shifting back towards the center. It needs to be in the center. We need campuses where students have really good intellectual debates and they have really great educational experiences. I think most university presidents actually agree with Ken on this issue. You know what the universities in this country have done over the last 75 years? They have contributed disproportionately, not only to educating the next generation, but to economic prosperity, to our national security, and to the well-being of society in healthcare and other areas. So I think we've been true to our mission. The important thing for us moving forward is to stay true to that core mission: educate the next generation, create knowledge to benefit society, and have economic and cultural impact. I mean, I'm a bit older than you are. When I applied to Princeton, the conversation with the admissions officer was, "If you come to Princeton, what great thing will you do for our country?" That needs to be the ethos of higher education. It's not four years of fun. It's not four years of an entitlement. It's four years preparing a young man or a young woman to make a difference in America. Just real quick, what's going to be your metric of success for the Miami campus in particular, in this new approach? Look, I would love to see the continuation of the success that happens here in Pittsburgh. This university is single-handedly re-architecting Pittsburgh to be a great city of the 21st century. You know, Ed Glaeser at Harvard has done a fair amount of work on the importance of universities; great cities are anchored by great universities. To complement the universities that exist in Miami today with one of the greatest universities in the world is a gift that will be one of the highest-impact gifts I will make in my lifetime. We talked about Miami quite a bit, but let's not forget that Ken is also making a historical gift to the Pittsburgh campus on day one to support our most important priorities, as well as to support our number one computer science school in the country, which is going to be named the Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science. President Jahanian and Ken, both of you, thank you so much for taking the time.

Ex-NVIDIA Engineer: Why AI Is Running Out of Copper

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(73.8K)·33:19·2.0K views·2026-09-30
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Yannick De KoninckEngineer, Thema Herwig Van HoveInvestor, Thema
TL;DRAs AI models exceed single-GPU capacity, traditional copper interconnects are hitting physical limits, driving the industry toward optical interconnects and silicon photonics.
Thema's Herwig Van Hove and Yannick De Koninck are tackling this infrastructure bottleneck by building...

TL;DR

As AI models exceed single-GPU capacity, traditional copper interconnects are hitting physical limits, driving the industry toward optical interconnects and silicon photonics. Thema's Herwig Van Hove and Yannick De Koninck are tackling this infrastructure bottleneck by building large-scale silicon photonics manufacturing in Europe.

The Infrastructure Bottleneck: Why Copper is Reaching Its Limit

  • GPU-to-GPU Communication Limits:
    • As AI models grow too large for a single GPU, multiple GPUs must be interconnected.
    • Copper interconnects are running out of steam because modern AI workloads require higher data transfer rates and bandwidth.
  • Shift to Optical Interconnects:
    • The transition from copper to optical interconnects and silicon photonics is necessary to support the immense data throughput required by modern AI.
  • The AI Factory Architecture Shift:
    • Network Topology: Traditional hyper-scale data centers relied on a deep hierarchical tree of switches for Google searches. Modern AI infrastructure requires a much flatter network with direct connections between switches and GPUs to handle high-volume, latency-sensitive GPU-to-GPU traffic (including agentic AI tool calls).
  • The Energy Crisis:
    • Existential Threat: Data center energy consumption is the number one issue keeping CTOs awake at night because local power grids cannot deliver sufficient power.
    • Grid Constraints: Google spent three years negotiating with Amsterdam before moving plans to Belgium due to a lack of power grid capacity. Upgrading grids or building new power plants cannot keep pace with AI demand.
    • Photonics as the Killer App: Energy consumption is the ultimate killer app for silicon photonics, a technology 20 to 30 years in the making.

Market Dynamics & The Supply Chain Deficit

  • Explosive Market Growth:
    • BlackRock's Tony Kim estimates a $10 trillion industry rebuild, with data centers being heavily impacted due to outdated, water- and energy-inefficient infrastructure.
    • NVIDIA made massive investments in 2024 and 2025, investing 4x the total market size in the first 6 months of the year into companies like Lumentum and Coherent to push the market toward photonics.
  • Supply Chain Bottlenecks:
    • Component Scarcity: While foundries (TSMC, GlobalFoundries, Tower) handle front-end silicon photonics wafers, final chips require lasers (produced by Lumentum, Coherent) and raw substrates, which face severe supply limitations.
    • Manufacturing Challenges: Tool builders are not producing nearly enough tools, and existing tools suffer from low throughput, efficiency, and yield.
    • Geopolitical Divide: The East-West divide creates additional friction in securing raw materials and substrates.

Thema's European Manufacturing Strategy

  • Founding and Infrastructure:
    • Thema acquired a large-scale European foundry that went out of business, gaining ready-to-go cleanrooms and complex infrastructure that would normally take 3 to 5 years to permit and build.
    • The location is strategically positioned 20 kilometers away from IMEC (Interuniversity Microelectronics Center), a world-leading research center and birthplace of silicon photonics.
  • Talent Acquisition:
    • Despite initial skepticism about hiring, Thema successfully attracted top-tier talent from companies like Amazon Web Services (AWS), TSMC, Lam Research, and NVIDIA.
    • The board includes three senior figures from IMEC, including the visionary professor who founded the photonics department at the University of Gent (which produced 35% of the 4,000 peer-reviewed global photonics white papers between 2000 and 2015).
  • Overcoming Misconceptions:
    • American tier-one customers initially questioned Thema's ability to secure funding, talent, machines, and substrates while meeting aggressive timelines due to the stereotype that Europe is slow and committee-driven.
    • Thema is intentionally adopting a "fail fast" and agile corporate culture to counter traditional European bureaucracy.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Production Timeline: Thema plans to start production in 2027 and work toward a full ramp-up by 2028 — *Herwig Van Hove & Yannick De Koninck*.

Notable Quotes

"I moved back to IMAC where I worked on laser integration for silicon photonics for a while and then five years ago I joined Nvidia back then when I still had to explain to people what Nvidia actually was." — Yannick De Koninck
"Every deep tech paradigm shift needs a killer app for photonics. More than 20 years in the making, 30 years in the making in on the science side, energy consumption by data centers and AI is that killer app." — Herwig Van Hove
"I realized that this was a once in a-lifetime opportunity for me to join a mission that's going to solve some of the core problems that are slowing down probably one of the biggest or the biggest technological revolution that I will witness in my lifetime. So I decided to leave the golden palace and jump onto the pirate ship and set sail." — Yannick De Koninck
Welcome to Sorcery. The potential of photonics, the technology to interconnect these GPUs, global payroll and HR platform, 8 billion operational data points, scaleups over startups. You'd rather have a million of something and one of anything. Building the intelligence layer through defense. There isn't enough power. There's not enough memory chips. We're at the end of physics of what we can still do. Yannik and Herwig, welcome to Sorcery and Strikes annual gala. We're here in Monaco and we're going to go deep into photonics and what you're building with TheMA. But before we get into that, we're going to go into a long conversation on photonics. So Yannik, I'd love to start with your background and then Herwig, we'll get into yours. How did you get into photonics? So I was one of these nerdy kids who locked himself up in his bedroom playing Lego all day. So naturally, I went to engineering school and that was 20 years ago. And back then, Silicon Photonics was this niche upcoming branch in engineering with no prospect for any job at all. So I decided to jump on that ship. I did my PhD in that field and then by that time I had the opportunity to move to San Diego to join a startup company there that was trying to commercialize silicon photonics, one of the first companies to do that and to successfully do that. I moved back to IMEC where I worked on laser integration for silicon photonics for a while and then five years ago I joined Nvidia back then when I still had to explain to people what Nvidia actually was. And so we started from scratch, built the silicon photonics technology there into a product-ready maturity, and now I'm starting at TheMA. How did you get involved? So I've been an investor most of my life, turned operator very late, but I've been focused on deep tech for quite a while. And so I got involved in the fascinating world of photonics almost a decade ago and realized three or four years ago that the train was leaving the station and there were some gaping holes in the supply chain. That is what we're trying to solve with TheMA, realizing that you know this visionary choice of Yannik 20 years ago to study the field of silicon photonics was something that has put Belgium on the map and has created a talent base that's broad and deep. And I've spent my life selecting, honing, and nurturing talent, and so that's what we're going to hope and do with this. So let's lay out the state of the market and how chips have gotten to this point, why copper is going out of date, and why there's a focus on photonics and lasers. Can you explain a little bit of that and also why Europe? So, I think the market is the part that really dragged me into it. We saw a much bigger opportunity than what most people in the field were seeing. That was because we were talking to the CTOs of data centers and realized indeed what their need was and how photonics could help solve this. And we were listening very carefully when some of the gurus of the ecosystem like Andy Bechtolsheim were quoting numbers that far exceeded what even the most optimistic analysts were saying and we felt that they were very well informed and so that's what pushed us to do this. To answer your question about where we are today, if you think about AI, you think about GPUs, right? You think about these compute engines that actually run the models, but the models have gotten so large that they no longer fit on a single GPU. So what we need to do is we need to interconnect multiple GPUs together to run these models. Traditionally, that's been copper interconnects, but as these GPUs get faster, they need more data. They need more data at a faster rate and copper is running out of steam. That's why we're transitioning to optical interconnects, which bring much higher data transfer bandwidth. So I recently interviewed Tony Kim of BlackRock in Paris at the Raise AI summit and we were breaking down the entire industry. He says there's a $10 trillion rebuild that's happening and one of the most affected places for that spend is in these data centers because the current old infrastructure of a data center is not built for AI. It's completely inefficient. It takes a lot of water. It takes a lot of energy and the chips that are being used in those are just outdated and they cannot handle the parameter models that are in the market. So I would love to get an understanding from one of you the difference in architecture of those data centers and how they're transforming with this new build. If you compare a traditional hyperscale data center to a modern AI factory, one of the big differences is that there is way more traffic between different servers. If you think about your traditional Google search five years ago, it was basically your traffic is routed to one computer that finds the answer and sends that answer back. Now, if you think about modern AI infrastructure, the models no longer fit on a single GPU. So there's already GPU-to-GPU communication. But now in the age of agentic AI, there's a model that is doing a call to some tool which is on a different server that's returning the results to the GPU. So there's a lot of traffic within the data center which is very sensitive to latency—basically the time between when all these different calculations can run. What that means in reality is that you need a much flatter network. Instead of a really deep hierarchical tree of switches, you need a much flatter network. That means far more direct connections between switches and GPUs. So basically a much denser network than what we had in the traditional hyperscale. If you're asking a CTO of a data center what keeps you awake at night, the energy consumption is answer number one because it's existential to them. They don't know how to solve it. Their CEO is saying we need to grow, but the CTO is asking where, because nobody can deliver the grid. Google negotiated for three years with Amsterdam and had to move its plans to Belgium because they simply can't get the grid. And so obviously with the ambitious growth plans that they have, it requires a much more fundamental solution that can scale to the same size as the problem. There are many data centers and many more being planned; therefore, upgrading the grid or building new power plants cannot happen in the time that is required. That's where every deep tech paradigm shift needs a killer app for photonics. More than 20 or 30 years in the making on the science side, energy consumption by data centers and AI is that killer app. Now we get to where we are today, which is that's great, but nobody was prepared for the speed of change. The speed of change happened in November 2022 when ChatGPT came out; it went viral like nothing we'd ever seen before. With the growth of that came this requirement or this realization: okay, we need to switch to photonics. Nvidia took the lead in that. They had been talking about photonics for a long time, but they said we need to make the switch happen. In 2024 they were even louder about it. In 2025 they really pushed the market. In 2026 they showed the market what they meant with their investments in Lumentum and Coherent. They've invested 4x the total market size in the first 6 months of this year. Now everybody realizes the supply chain is not ready. The innovation is ready; the photonic scientists and designers have done a lot of work, but you need to work that all the way back to the beginning—from the raw material, through the tooling, to the infrastructure to build. I think that's where the pain point sits today and it's part of what we're trying to solve. So how many photonic companies are out there? I interviewed the Lumentum CEO Michael Hurlston, and even just looking at that company, they are spilling over with demand. Their stock went up a thousand times in a year. It's insane. He had just stepped in maybe a year and a half ago, so he really came in at an opportune time. But I'm curious what the state of the market looks like for the companies that are existing and how does it all work together? The shift in the size of the market needs to be understood. Photonics for years was an academic endeavor with some applications being prepared for in the supply chain at that scale. Now, all of a sudden, Nvidia steps in and says, "No, no, guys, this is much, much bigger." Obviously Lumentum and Coherent are the two leading companies in the West doing this; they're the only ones scaled at that size. If you look at the rest of the industry, the traditional players—the TSMCs, GlobalFoundries, Towers of this world—have made a tremendous effort investing in the part that fits in their production process, which is the front end, the silicon photonics. So making the wafers. But if you deliver a wafer to Nvidia, that's not what they ordered. They ordered a final chip. So there's a lot of other things required, lasers first and foremost, which is what Lumentum and others are making. That's going to have to scale by a lot, and that's challenging because the throughput of the machines is not at all where it needs to be. The efficiency and the yield are not what they're supposed to be, and the substrates—the raw material—are really cornered. The market is limited; there's only a handful of suppliers, and on top of all these problems you now have the East-West divide, which is getting quite problematic. In all of that, we think Europe is in a sweet spot because in Europe, you have industrial capacity and infrastructure that was available. That's what we bought. You have photonic talent that has been trained for decades. You have industrial talent that was available because all the big foundries closed. The combination of all of this, in addition to a desire for European sovereignty, makes us feel like we're in an ideal spot to do this from Europe. So let's go to the founding of the company and how you guys came together. What was that process like? We had been building the concept of a pure-play photonic foundry at a much larger scale. We were looking around, seeing that all the big guys were doing the front end of the process, and we were seeing a lot of innovation happening, but that was mostly university spinout scale. So there wasn't really an industrial player in the middle. As we were making plans in 2023 and 2024 to do this, there was I guess some serendipity involved in that a large-scale European foundry went out of business. So we took the occasion, we jumped on it, we bought it, and it delivered, I think, a very nice combination. One, it's 20 km away from IMEC, a world-leading research center with lots of talent. Two, it has lots of industrial talent and an infrastructure that was ready to go right at the time when everybody needs it. When you want to build a foundry of this size, you're talking about a three-to-five-year project. It's not just clean rooms; the infrastructure is quite complex. So having all of it ready to go in 2024 was a serendipitous event. And so that's where we jumped on it. We started talking to both sides at the same time: the industrial talent and the photonic talent. We said, "Look, Europe has successfully invested for 20 or 30 years in photonic innovation. This is the time for industrialization," and Yannik and others jumped on board instantly. Yannik, from a technical standpoint, where are you at? I was in a great spot at Nvidia, building the next generation of chips, and I wasn't planning to go anywhere. But when I started to talk to Herwig and I learned about the ambition of this enterprise and what he was planning to do, I realized that this was a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity for me to join a mission that's going to solve some of the core problems that are slowing down probably the biggest technological revolution that I will witness in my lifetime. So I decided to leave the golden palace and jump onto the pirate ship and set sail. Selfish question: what is it like at Nvidia? What have you learned there? Operating principles? It's an incredible company. It feels like a startup; it's very agile. The idea is to fail fast, so we start building stuff, and if it doesn't work, move to something new. In my experience, it's a very apolitical company. The org chart is just a piece of paper. If there's a project and you can contribute, you contribute to that project. Doesn't matter where you are in the org chart. And for people who don't know what IMEC is, can you explain that and where it fits in the stack? IMEC is the Interuniversity Microelectronics Centre. IMEC was one of the birthplaces of silicon photonics in the world and has grown to be the number one research center when it comes to advanced electronics technologies. Where are you guys at in terms of commercial progress to date? When are you going to start rolling out your customers? Our biggest challenge is the timeline customers are putting in front of us. The good news is that the volumes they're asking for even surpass our wildly ambitious projections, so that's certainly very positive. The constraints for us now—the whole market, Coherent, Lumentum and others—everybody is facing the same constraints. The tool builders are not building anywhere near enough tools, and the tools themselves need to be further improved for throughput, efficiency, and yield. And even more problematic are the substrates—the raw material. Here's the good news: as I said, Europe has been investing successfully in innovation. To answer your question, our timeline is to start producing next year in 2027 and work towards a full ramp-up by 2028. Our timelines are short, but Europe is wide awake and ready to go. We feel that's not just a slogan; we feel it every day. There's a lot of collaboration going on and we're finally starting to really work across borders. I'm very optimistic about Europe playing a significant role. Has it been hard to hire? That was the question I got most before I started because I was all alone. What Yannik didn't say is that he chose a niche field to study, but back then, you either went abroad to work or you became a banker. It was either or. If you read the website of the European Council on the European Chip Act, they say that they really want to prioritize photonics and focus on industrialization. It's almost like reading a definition of TheMA. It has to do with European sovereignty, but it's also that Europe realizes it has what it takes to not just play but actually win in this game. With the exception of Biscoff and chocolate, Belgium has no dominance like that in any other field. I'm curious about your answers to this: what do you think the biggest misconceptions are with European AI? We were a startup with two people at that point in time, so they shouldn't have been talking to us, but they were. They challenged us: "Can you find the funding? Can you find the talent? Can you find the machines and the substrates? And within all of that, how on earth are you going to respect the timelines we require?" The conception of Europe is that it's slow and committee-driven. What we're definitely incorporating in TheMA is, like Yannik said, this culture of fail-fast, making decisions, driving forward, and not expecting 100% perfect information. That may not be traditionally "European," but the world is globalized. We're awake, and we're going to take the best of American culture as well. I was in California last week, and when I drove from San Francisco airport to Santa Clara, there were all these billboards on AI. You can feel there's incredible excitement about AI in the US. I don't think many people who live there like those billboards, but you can feel the excitement. I think we have very similar excitement here in Europe. We may not be as vocal about it, but we're going to turn that excitement into reality. Is there as much hate for data centers here as there is in America? It's a huge media propaganda thing. Europe doesn't have the same network of data centers that the US has, and the regulations here are a lot stricter. I would almost flip it around; that's the opportunity for us. If we can help build the infrastructure that makes this whole business a lot more energy-efficient, then hopefully we will also start building out our own homegrown data center network in Europe, which may look very different than the mega hyperscalers that the US has built. Europe doesn't have the same aversion toward it because it simply doesn't have the problem; it was regulated before the problem arose. What are the timelines that you think upon? What are you most looking forward to? We're getting ready for production in 2027. What I'm looking most forward to is proving the incredible gaps that we're going to cover between lab and fab. We have the right team on board to do that. You're coming from a very competitive company. Do you bring that competition with you? Is that why this is so enticing? I think so. I like a challenge. Are there a lot of companies spinning out of Nvidia in Europe right now? Not that I'm aware of. No. As we close out, what is your hottest take right now? I think both of these are true: Europe is underrated and photonics are underrated. People are waking up to the potential of photonics, but it's just the start of a much larger manufacturing ecosystem that's going to exist. Europe is underrated because the innovation that has occurred here has not been commercialized here; whatever was good enough was taken away to Taiwan or the US. To build it here, Europe has done this once before—it's a long time ago, but it's been a 40-year success story called ASML. I think Europe is ready for more stories like that. My hot take is: if you look at an AI factory today, half of that is GPUs, but the other half is the technology to interconnect these GPUs. There's a huge opportunity for optics and photonics there, and we have to tap into that. Amazing. Great place to end it. Herwig, thank you so much for a nice little deep dive on photonics. Thank you. Griffin Gives $3 Billion to Carnegie Mellon for Miami Campus

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·11:36·2.3K views·2026-09-30
Ken GriffinCEO, Citadel Farnam JahanianPresident, Carnegie Mellon University Lisa AbramowiczAnchor, Bloomberg
TL;DR"This is an investment in excellence.
Citadel founder Ken Griffin is donating a record-breaking $3 billion to Carnegie Mellon University—doubling his total lifetime contributions to roughly $5.7 billion—with a significant portion funding a new CMU campus in Miami designed around a pioneering,...

TL;DR: Citadel founder Ken Griffin is donating a record-breaking $3 billion to Carnegie Mellon University—doubling his total lifetime contributions to roughly $5.7 billion—with a significant portion funding a new CMU campus in Miami designed around a pioneering, interdisciplinary model for higher education.

The Record-Breaking Gift & Investment in Excellence

  • Historic Donation Scale: Ken Griffin committed $3 billion to Carnegie Mellon University, marking the largest single gift ever made to a US university and bringing his total giving to approximately $5.7 billion.
  • Investment in Excellence: Ken Griffin framed the donation as an investment in national leadership, citing CMU's top-tier standing in computer science, artificial intelligence, cybersecurity, and robotics.
  • Pittsburgh Impact & Naming: A portion of the funds will support the Pittsburgh campus on day one, including naming the top-ranked computer science school the Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science.

A New Model for Higher Education in Miami

  • Pioneering a Miami Campus: President Farnam Jahanian and Ken Griffin detailed plans to establish a new CMU campus in Miami, modeled after how the university helped drive Pittsburgh's industrial renaissance.
  • Ecosystem Transformation: Ken Griffin predicted that the Miami branch will attract hundreds of technology companies, startups, and career opportunities to the region.
  • Grand Challenges Over Silos: President Farnam Jahanian explained that the campus structure will abandon traditional departments in favor of organizing around grand societal challenges, such as:
    • Human health
    • National security and cybersecurity
    • Innovation, energy, and climate resiliency

Balancing Technical Rigor and Human Skills

  • ROI and Return to Core Mission: Ken Griffin highlighted that US News rankings place CMU #3 in the country for educational return on investment and #14 overall, arguing that universities must focus on tangible student outcomes rather than "four years of fun."
  • Interdisciplinary Synthesis: President Jahanian and Ken Griffin emphasized that the future requires well-rounded students who combine technical and scientific knowledge with critical thinking, problem-solving, and communication skills.
  • Humanities Integration: Ken Griffin and President Jahanian noted CMU's deep roots in the humanities, pointing to achievements like 70 Tony Award winners from its School of Drama (founded in 1914) and alumnus Andy Warhol.

Cultural Shift on American Campuses

  • Restoring the Narrative: Ken Griffin stated that higher education lost its narrative to issues that do not matter to the nation's future prosperity, but observed that the cultural pendulum is shifting back toward the center.
  • The Ethos of Higher Education: Ken Griffin argued that colleges must return to the foundational ethos of preparing young men and women to serve the country, run businesses, and solve tangible problems.

Notable Quotes
