Horizon Robotics CEO: Car Chips, Mind-Off Driving and China's Speed | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(132.0K)·47:05·5.8K views·2026-09-30
Nicolai TangenCEO, Norges Bank Investment Management Kai YuFounder and CEO, Horizon Robotics
TL;DRHorizon Robotics founder and CEO Dr.
Kai Yu explains how his 11-year-old "Wintel"-style computing platform powers one in three intelligent vehicles in China, detailing the intense competitive market, autonomous driving timelines, and the inevitable convergence of cars and...

TL;DR

Horizon Robotics founder and CEO Dr. Kai Yu explains how his 11-year-old "Wintel"-style computing platform powers one in three intelligent vehicles in China, detailing the intense competitive market, autonomous driving timelines, and the inevitable convergence of cars and humanoid robotics.

Company Overview & Positioning

  • Core Business: Dr. Kai Yu describes Horizon Robotics as the computing platform provider for generic robots, currently focused 95-100% on passenger vehicles by supplying chips and software spanning camera perception, trajectory planning, and control.
  • Market Share & Customer Base:
    • Supplies chips and software to one in three intelligent cars on Chinese roads (up from one in four previously).
    • Major Chinese original equipment manufacturer (OEM) customers include BYD, Geely, Chery, Changan, and Great Wall.
    • Global OEM customers include Volkswagen, Toyota, and Suzuki.
  • Competitive Landscape: Competes with Mobileye, Huawei, Black Sesame, Qualcomm, MediaTek, Texas Instruments, Renesas, and NXP. Yu notes that the automotive computing space in China has consolidated around three primary players: Nvidia, Huawei, and Horizon Robotics.
  • The "Wintel" Strategy: Deliberately chose never to build a car, instead modeling Horizon Robotics after Microsoft and Intel ("a Vintel") to enable all car brands and the broader robotics ecosystem rather than competing with them.
  • Company Scale: Employs around 2,000 people, the vast majority being software and hardware engineers operating under a high-ownership, flexible-hour culture.

Market Dynamics & Supply Chain Intensity

  • China's Hyper-Competitive Car Market: Describes the domestic market as "crazy," noting there are 60 to 70 different car brands and that more than 580 new car models were launched in the first half of the year alone.
  • Development Cycle Discrepancy:
    • Initiating a new chip and solution generation takes 3 to 4 years to ready for car makers.
    • Securing a nomination requires a production cycle of 2 years for Chinese OEMs, compared to 5 years for global OEMs.
    • Total cycle time spans 5 to 8 years from start to finished platform.
  • China's Supply Chain Advantage: Yu highlights China as a "wonderland for supply chain" due to its rich ecosystem, geographic proximity ("taking the entire China as one village"), and speed, allowing innovations to be realized rapidly through local partners.
  • The High-Barrier Paradox: Acknowledges that automotive is "terrible business" requiring heavy investment with very slow returns, but argues that these mission-critical safety and quality standards create an extremely high entry barrier that filters out casual VC-funded newcomers.

Autonomous Driving Timelines & Robotics Convergence

  • Three-Stage Autonomous Roadmap: Reaffirms milestones established in January of the previous year to measure progress for consumers:
    • Hands-off: Target by the end of 2028 (currently enabled by the Horizon Superdrive software stack running on the Journey 6T SOC).
    • Eyes-off: Target by the end of 2030 (requiring larger knowledge bases, test mileage, and statistical safety proof).
    • Minds-off: Target by the end of 2035 (allowing passengers to sleep while the vehicle operates).
  • Robotics Convergence:
    • Horizon Robotics is already a top-two player in generic robotics computing in China, supplying chip solutions to humanoid robot companies.
    • Yu predicts that car technology and humanoid robotics will eventually converge into a unifying computing platform because cars fundamentally function as moving robots.
    • Foresees meaningful volumes of humanoid robots in home service and elderly care within 5 years (optimistic projection).
    • Argues that winning the automotive supply chain directly translates to winning the robotics supply chain due to shared actuators and sensors (like lasers and radars) optimized by high-volume car manufacturing.

Philosophy & Personal Leadership

  • Zen & The Power of Now: Emphasizes a Zen Buddhist philosophy of focus—working entirely when working, sleeping entirely when sleeping, and letting go of failures immediately through extreme optimism.
  • Vision of Future Cities: Envisions cities where autonomous fleets operate as coordinated systems rather than competing traffic, giving human freedom back by eliminating tedious two-hour commutes and transforming cars into moving living spaces.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Dr. Kai Yu predicts all passenger vehicles in China will achieve 100% hands-free operation by the end of 2028.
  • Dr. Kai Yu predicts vehicles will achieve 100% eyes-off capability by the end of 2030.
  • Dr. Kai Yu predicts vehicles will achieve 100% minds-off capability (allowing sleeping while driving) by the end of 2035.
  • Dr. Kai Yu predicts meaningful volumes of humanoid robots will emerge for home service and elderly care within 5 years (optimistic timeline).

Notable Quotes

"In some sense this is terrible business you know, heavy investment, very slow return, but in my view sometimes terrible business means a great business because that's the entry barrier for all the other newcomers is extremely high." — Dr. Kai Yu
"The essence of robotics is to give the freedom, give the choice back to the human... In that [traffic] situation you are slave of the car because you don't have choice." — Dr. Kai Yu
"When you are working you just focus on working. When you are sleeping just sleep, and then I focus the power of now." — Dr. Kai Yu
In some sense this is terrible business, you know, heavy investment, very slow return. But in my view, sometimes a terrible business means a great business because the entry barrier for all the other newcomers is extremely high. Everybody, I'm Nikolai Tangen, the CEO of the Norwegian Sovereign Wealth Fund. Ten years ago, we all assumed self-driving cars would come out of Silicon Valley. But today, the most advanced cars on the road are Chinese and Western car makers are going to China to buy the technology. Nobody sits closer to the shift than my guest, Yukai, who is the founder and CEO of Horizon Robotics, which supplies the chips and software that let Chinese cars drive themselves. He was one of China's leading AI scientists before he became an entrepreneur, and few people can explain better how China got there and where it goes next. So, Kai, welcome. Hi, Nikolai. Let's just start with a simple question. What does Horizon Robotics do? Well, essentially, what we are doing is the computing platform for all generic robots, including self-driving cars. But of course, our current business is probably, you know, 95 to nearly 100% focusing on cars—passenger vehicles. So, if I sit in a car that you supply, what does your chip do? Well, essentially, it's running the software all the way from camera perception to trajectory planning and control. So, essentially, it's kind of like a brain for all the cars. Now, your chips run roughly one out of four cars in China. Just tell me about the... One out of three, I think. That's our latest track record. Thank you. Well done. Who are your customers? Well, you know, the major Chinese OEMs like BYD, Geely, Chery, Changan, Great Wall, and also some of the global OEMs like Volkswagen, Toyota, Suzuki. So, quite some local domestic and global players. Who do you compete with? Oh, very good question. I think like Mobileye, you know, and also Huawei and quite a few domestic players like Black Sesame. I'm not sure if you have ever heard of that. And Qualcomm and Nvidia, Texas Instruments, you know, Renesas, NXP. So, actually, a lot of global and China technology companies. Why are you the best? Oh, well, a very good question. I think it's really because we are very focused. You know, we focus on this single one thing to enable autonomous driving and all the way to robotics. We are not doing anything else. So, I think the intensity of focus is one thing. The other thing I would say is fundamentally our vision and mission. Because if you look at the name of our company, you know, 11 years ago when we founded this company, we named our company Horizon Robotics. Back 11 years ago in China, there was no such a business at all. And even the EV business was just starting. Right, and we had this vision which was not an existing opportunity. We envisioned in the future the world will be full of all kinds of different robots, you know, working in a farming field, manufacturer assembly line, and the logistic warehouse, you know, every house, home service, and elderly care, and also, of course, self-driving cars. So, we envisioned there should be a unifying platform, just like Windows and Intel did for personal computing, and just like ARM and Android are doing for mobile computing. So, we envisioned in the future there is going to be such a kind of computing platform which is generic for all kinds of robotics working across different vertical domains. When exactly did the vision hit you? Were you in the shower or were you walking in the mountains or just what? Well, that's a very good question. I think you are right. I think the vision actually hit me—I didn't just find this opportunity or this vision. I have been working in machine learning for over 30 years, you know, since my early days as a junior student in college. By chance, I read a book about machine learning and I was so amazed that it's so fantastic to develop a computer algorithm that one day can be like a human brain, getting smarter and smarter over experience by learning more and more from data. So, then I decided to devote my whole career into this single subject, machine learning. That was for my thesis for my undergraduate and then my thesis for my master and also for my PhD. And also for my first job back to my days in Siemens, and later to Silicon Valley, I joined NEC Labs in Silicon Valley, and then later joined the Baidu, and then later founded the Horizon Robotics. So, I changed my locations and I changed different roles from, you know, individual researcher to a team leader to a head of research labs and later became a founder of a technology company. So, I changed my roles, but there's one thing remains never changed, which is my mission and my vision: to develop machine learning algorithms to make machines getting smarter and smarter. Two companies that we've had on the podcast, Xpeng and Xiaomi, they also make phones and cars in addition to chips. So, how do you... why did you decide never to build a car? I always position ourselves as going to be like a Microsoft and Intel, not for PCs but for robots. And I think a platform—an enabling platform—is a much more interesting positioning, not just about the business model but also in terms of the impact you are going to make to the whole world. For example, back to the days of the personal computing era, Windows and Intel, they were not just helping or enabling Lenovo or Dell; they actually helped the entire mankind get into a new kind of technology civilization or a new area of technology, which is the personal computer, to enable all the white-collar people to increase their productivity dramatically. It's such an amazing impact. Xiaomi, they actually help one brand to become very appealing and very attractive to consumers, but I want to enable and help all the brands, all the cars. Is it difficult to build a car? Yeah, of course. But you have the technology, you have the chips. So if I ask you to build a car, how long would it take you? You know, I wanted to achieve "Horizon Inside"—I think that's a much bigger dream, much more interesting for me. I don't want to be a car maker, you know, and I want to help more car brands to become more attractive. Now, the Chinese car market is kind of brutally competitive. What is driving the Chinese car market just now? Is it price, intelligence, or just what is it? Well, the current Chinese car market is really crazy. I think you don't see any market like it here. There are, I don't know, probably 60 to 70 different car brands, and for the first half of this year, more than 580 new car models were launched. It's an extremely tough market, but I think the market is really driven by competition. But on the other hand, it's also driven by consumer demand. Consumers are really expecting more and more smart features—automatic parking, autonomous driving, and more fancy infotainment systems. I think all of those factors are driving the market to be not just competitive, but also very innovative. What does it do to Chinese manufacturers when you have such strong competition at home and then you go out to, let's say, Europe? Is it easy-peasy to compete relatively? I think we position ourselves... first, we are not competing with any car makers. We are actually enabling them. We are helping them. We are building strong partnerships with all the car makers and all the ecosystem partners, including the automotive suppliers, for example, Bosch, Denso, and many other global and local suppliers. Our philosophy is really to make friends and build long-term trust with our customers and partners. Essentially, this is fundamentally in the gene of our company. So, we don't have too much culture from the competition perspective, but more from what kind of value we can create. But luckily, if you look at our business, fundamentally compared to those car makers, we are not so crowded. If you look at China, I think pretty much the three top players for automotive computing have consolidated to Nvidia, Huawei, and Horizon Robotics—not very crowded. Partially because we choose the position where this business has an extremely high entry barrier. It's not like a bunch of guys having VC funding can easily get into the business, because this is automotive. Automotive means mission-critical, and it has to have the highest standard for quality and safety. Let's say what we are doing is chip design for automotive, right? It's not like consumer-type silicon. It has to go through all the very rigorous and tough qualification and testing processes. So, normally, if we initiate a new project, a new generation of product, it will take three to four years to get the product, the chip, and the solution ready for car makers. And then, if lucky enough, we win a new nomination from a car maker, we need to go through the cycle of production for the car platforms, and that's going to be another three to five years in China. For global OEMs, they are slower, typically five years. So, then think about it, it's going to be at least five to eight years for the cycle. In some sense, this is a terrible business—heavy investment, very slow return. But in my view, sometimes a terrible business means a great business, because that entry barrier for all other newcomers is extremely high. And then I think a good business is something where you can sleep well overnight, and that's my opinion. Talking about sleeping well and the autonomous journey, you split it between "hands off," "eyes off," and "mind off." These are kind of the three steps that a car will go through. And in January last year, you put dates on them. So, we'll have eyes off in three years, I'm sorry, hands off in three years, we'll have eyes off in five, and mind off in 10. Just where are we today? Well, we are making very good progress. In the industry, there's a sort of standard from Level 2, Level 3, Level 4, Level 5, but that's really from the engineering standpoint. For the consumers, they have no clue what is Level 3. So, I always try to connect our technology and the product to something where my mom, at least, can understand. So that's my way to interpret the technology stages: hands-free, hands-off, eyes-off, and minds-off. So what do you tell your mom just now? Right now, we are making very good progress to achieve fully hands-off. I made that statement last year, right? So that means by the end of 2028, we are going to achieve fully hands-off, then by the end of 2030 to achieve fully eyes-off, and then by the end of 2035, it's going to be 100% minds-off—basically you can sleep in your car. I think we are making very good progress. If you look at our product, our latest product—the software stack called the Horizon Superdrive running on our Journey 6T SoC—currently it's essentially something you can feel completely hands-free. It's just like a new, smarter version of automatic transmission. If you remember, over the last 20 years, the whole car industry evolved from manual transmission, where you need to control all the gears, into automatic transmission. But now, I call this getting into the smarter version of automatic transmission. Basically, you just need to stay watching ahead, and nothing else needs your care. What is still lacking? What critical element is still left here? Well, I think we need a much bigger knowledge base to test, and we need to have better numbers for statistics to prove that the technology is safe enough to claim "eyes-off." So I think we are already making progress to approach the stage of hands-off, but I think we need much bigger mileage to test and prove eyes-off. Do you build your own AI models? Sure, the self-driving model, yeah. Remember, we position ourselves as the "Intel"—not just Intel, we position ourselves as a "v-Intel." And I think v-Intel is really an amazing business model. And where is the future breakthrough going to come from? Is it the model, the data, or the chips? Well, it's a combination of three factors together. I think this is a typical formula for today's AI business. I think you need to have a much bigger compute running locally on the vehicle, and also you need to be continuously collecting bigger and bigger data, but also meaningful data. It's not just quantity, but also quality. That means you need to collect many, many of the meaningful corner cases. And I think also you need bigger and bigger models in terms of the number of parameters. How many people are you now in the company? Around 2,000. What do they do? I think we are a very technology-savvy company. So most of our employees are engineers; they are either software engineers or hardware engineers, and they are working extremely hard. Because you don't produce the chips, you outsource the production, right? Exactly. We are like a typical, for the hardware side, we are a typical fabless company. We design the chip, but we don't manufacture the chip; we outsource to our partners. And also, we develop the software—the end-to-end neural network for self-driving. What does "working hard" mean? You know, we are a typical technology culture. We don't have the rules or the policy that you need to come to work at a certain time. Actually, most of our engineers come to work around 10 a.m., and they freely organize their working time. There's no rule like, "Okay, before which time you are not supposed to leave the office." So there's no such thing. I think the sense of ownership of our engineers is very high. What do you think, on average, how long of days do you think they work? Actually, I have no clue, and quite some of our engineers are even working from home. We don't measure that by working hours. I do know some of the China companies measure by working hours; we never measure that. We just care about what kind of goal we can achieve. When you say that the model time in China—the cycle time—is two years versus five years in Europe, what explains the difference? As far as I know, because I work with so many Chinese OEMs, they are working extremely hard. They have a very strong sense of competition. That urges them to try all the ways to improve the process. They work really hard; for example, they don't have summer vacations like 30 or 40 working days off during the summer. What's the key to innovation? How do you create a really innovative culture? I think first of all, we are a company driven by the vision. We are not opportunity-driven, and we are not competition-driven. We work on that because we really want to push the boundary of the entire society and the industry. I think this self-motivation is very important. That means we don't want to be the same as any other company; we don't want to do the sorts of things other companies are doing. That explains why our business essentially is not so crowded in China. This is actually a very real phenomenon, because in China, if any business looks even somewhat interesting, typically hundreds of companies jump in to compete. It's crazy. Look at the today's AI and embodied AI business—so many companies and also so many car companies. I think we are working on something which we believe in, but not necessarily something other people love to jump into. The fact that you have all these companies jumping into these sectors, what does it mean in terms of the supply chain and the various choices you have as a manufacturer? For us, we just choose the best to work on. We don't have the luxury to be... I mean, some things we need to be very innovative, for example, the chip architecture, the compiler, the algorithm. But some things we are just so much respecting the tradition, because this really means life and death—it's mission-critical. If any car accident happens, it's not just impacting the driver or passenger; it's sometimes a public safety issue. So we need to be very religiously respectful of the tradition. We just choose the best partner to work with. What advantage do you think it is for Chinese auto manufacturers now to have that breadth and depth of supply chain which we don't have in Europe? I work very closely with many global automotive companies like Bosch, Denso, Volkswagen. I talked to them about that. It turned out that actually, over the last two decades or so, China became a country where the supply chain of components and technologies is so rich. This is exactly what one of my friends, a German, told me. He thinks China nowadays is like a wonderland for supply chain, because no matter what new ideas you have, you can always find the partners and the suppliers in China to either supply the technology component to you or work with you to fulfill your requirement. The speed is incredible. I think that gives China an overall very fantastic environment to innovate. The overall ecosystem is there; you don't need to write an email to somewhere else. You just make a phone call, send a WeChat message, or just take a flight from Beijing to Shenzhen—just three hours. Nowadays, myself and all of my friends, we travel so frequently within China; we take the entire China as one village. When you see a client of yours such as Volkswagen laying off 100,000 people, what are your reflections? Well, we actually position ourselves as a strong partner to Volkswagen and many other global companies because we don't face any competition in Europe. If you look at Europe, there are semiconductor companies like Infineon, NXP, and STMicroelectronics. We are not competing with them because they have their focus on, for example, analog chips and MCUs. Those are their strengths, but we are working on something like computing for cars, which is exactly what they are not doing. In that sense, we find a very strong synergy to work together. In my view, I think both European friends and Chinese need to change the mindset. In the past several decades, the general pattern was global companies trying to make money from China, and typically for automotive, nowadays it's getting very difficult. And now there could be some other mindset, like, "Oh, Chinese companies now have a very competitive product, we need to get into Europe to take their market share and make money." That's also not healthy. I think we all need to embrace a new mindset, which is a win-win mindset. The new opportunity is for us to work together, to explore new markets, to launch more competitive products, and to create value for the global market. That's the kind of mindset I share deeply with many of my friends in Germany, France, and other countries. Your company is called Horizon Robotics. How far is a car from a robot now? Still a little bit of a long way to go, but not that far. As I said, in two years, by the end of 2028, all the passenger vehicles in China will be hands-free. It's already kind of a semi-robot, right? And what is your business going to be in the robotics business? Actually, we are already a leading player in China, one of the top two players for generic robotics computing. We are enabling a lot of robotics companies by supplying computing solutions. What's the overlap in technology and systems between self-driving cars and humanoid robotics, for instance? That's a very good question, and I believe eventually the two technologies will really converge and become a unifying computing platform. Cars will become robots, and there's no big difference. The cars will sense the environment, understand the intention of the pedestrians and traffic participants, understand the gestures of a policeman, and make decisions in sophisticated traffic environments. That is fundamentally no different from the computing patterns of other robots. So eventually, it will converge. Tell me about the speed of development of humanoid robots in China now. Well, you know what, it's crazy again—there are like hundreds of such companies. It's so intense, but I think all of them are still a long way to go. If you look at autonomous driving, Horizon Robotics was probably the first autonomous driving startup in China. We have been working here for 11 years, and now we are top two. For humanoid robots, it will take many years. What do you do inside the robot? What does your software do? We supply chip solutions. For robots, we are not so much into the software; we are supplying chips to the robot companies. When do you think we'll have meaningful volumes of humanoid robots? Five years, if optimistic. I know I'm a little bit conservative compared to many other guys, but I try to be objective. As a CEO and founder, I'm basically a technology guy, so I'm a little bit more conservative and objective. What will you personally use them for? Well, I think the number one scenario is home service. For each family, we enjoy all the fun things, but we don't enjoy the tedious work, like cleaning tables after dinner. Sometimes we like cooking, but we don't like cleaning. I have a theory that divorce rates will go down when you don't have to do boring things anymore. Exactly. Or take out the rubbish or walk the dog. There's another thing: China is facing a very terrible aging society issue. It's coming so quickly, quite unexpectedly. The issue is getting worse compared to Europe and even Japan. So, I think, what about elderly care? What about the lack of labor? This is going to be a huge social and economic challenge for China. What's the overlap in supply chain between self-driving cars and robotics? I think almost the same. If you win the EV race, you'll win the robotics race. That is fundamentally what I believe. My argument is, if you are a winner in the automotive supply chain, you are going to be a winner in robotics. If you look at the actuators, all the mechanical things, the sensors like lasers and radars, currently they are applied to cars. Because the car business is a big volume, through competition, all the suppliers will continuously improve the cost structure and the performance, and make it very compact. So, that's going to put those guys in a very strong position for the robotics business, just like many of the companies who were winners for the mobile phone supply chain later became extremely competitive for the generic IoT business. Stupid question, but where does that leave the race between China and the US in robotics? I don't like this kind of view to look at the game. I don't know if this zero-sum game is the truth. If China is very strong for manufacturing, then if the robotics industry in the US works with the Chinese ecosystem, they could become more innovative. They could innovate at a much faster pace. If they only rely on their own supply chain, they will end up with slower technology innovation. So, I think it really makes sense to always find the synergy to work together and create value for the global community. So what is a city going to look like in 10 to 20 years' time? I'm a huge fan of ancient Greek culture and history. In July, I was in Athens—I've been there many times. It was a very golden age where people really enjoyed free thinking and free exchange of ideas. There were so many inspiring philosophers who made a huge impact on human civilization, and they were free of labor work. I think civilization is so much related to the freedom of humans. If we have all the robots to do all the tedious and sometimes dangerous work, then we can have more time—like you and me right now—chatting, exchanging ideas, sometimes hanging around for other things. More time for entertainment, more time for research, more time for arts, and more time for outing. That would be great. If you look at today's traffic jams in Beijing and Shanghai, it's terrible. A one-way commute from home to office, you're stuck in traffic for almost two hours, and you waste two hours of your life doing nothing because you need 100% focus on steering the wheel. Are you the master of the car? No. In that situation, you are a slave of the car because you don't have a choice. The essence of robotics is to give freedom and give the choice back to the human, so the human can make a choice to do something or not do something. And what is the town going to look like physically? Even today in China, housing prices from district to district are quite different. Maybe in 10 years, the housing price won't be so different because essentially every car is like a moving house. You have much more freedom and you don't have such anxiety about distance. I think the city will be drastically different. Also, traffic—today people are competing and fighting for lanes and parking lots. If everything is self-driving, the efficiency of the roads will be much higher because all the cars are coordinated rather than competing against each other. I think the efficiency of traffic will be 10 times improved, and the environment will be much nicer, with much less noise, stress, and anxiety. I think the city will be much better. Take me back one second to your Athens visit. When you're in Athens, what do you do? Museums. And I also visit all the islands. So you go up to the Acropolis and you stand there... I was kind of stunned. I was so amazed. I think culture means everything. Human thinking is unbounded, and essentially we can do something much greater than what we can imagine today. In some sense, I think it's a shame that today in this world we are still doing so many terrible things and making the world a huge mess. We are not doing anything greater than the ancient Greeks. Personal question. What's your biggest personal failure in life? To be honest, I'm not a very pessimistic guy. If I ever encountered any frustration or failure, I always turn to a new page and I almost immediately forget. I think forgetting bad things is probably a good ability. I'm an insanely optimistic guy, and I also care about the power of now. I'm very good at following the philosophy of Zen—the Chinese/Asian Buddhist philosophy. What's the key to the Zen philosophy for you? Happiness is so simple. One of the great monks in the history of Zen said that many people have a miserable life just because when they are supposed to sleep, they think about work, and when they are working, they are thinking about sleeping. The sense of happiness is very simple: when you are working, just focus on working; when you are sleeping, just sleep. I focus on the power of now because I'm running Horizon Robotics; I just focus on this, and nothing else, nothing more, nothing less. What's something about you that most people don't know? There's one thing I'm not very satisfied about myself: my weight. I just have too good an appetite. I love food so much, so this is really something not so good. What do you read? Recently, I'm reading a book about the story of three companies: Microsoft, Intel, and Apple. It's about all the decisions and strategies they've been making to create the era of personal computing. It's called "Strategic Thinking." What's your advice to young people? Doing what you love and loving what you're doing are probably equally important. Many people always dream to do what they love, but in reality, you are not always so lucky. Sometimes you need to accept, and sometimes you need to accommodate. It's not pessimistic, because enjoying whatever you have right now is also a very good attitude. Sometimes you love what you are supposed to do, or what you are requested to do, and then maybe this whole thing can become something you really love. I think we need to have that kind of attitude: love what we do and do what we love. Well, I think that's a wonderful place to end. A big thank you. What an incredible story, what an incredible company. I love your life philosophy. There is so much to take away from here. So, a big thank you. Thank you, Nikolai. I really hope you liked the episode. If you did, please like it, subscribe, and put on the notifications.

How Sam Altman Actually Uses AI to Run OpenAI and His Life

Every(47.8K)·33:10·2026-09-30
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI Dan ShipperHost, The Every Podcast
TL;DRSam Altman discusses how OpenAI's new "Dot" persistent agent protects his morning creative time, why developer demand for AI remains deeply human-centered, and how OpenAI is betting on a decentralized "Renaissance" for builders rather than a single corporate monopoly.
Latent...

TL;DR

Sam Altman discusses how OpenAI's new "Dot" persistent agent protects his morning creative time, why developer demand for AI remains deeply human-centered, and how OpenAI is betting on a decentralized "Renaissance" for builders rather than a single corporate monopoly.

The Future of Engineering & Human-Centered AI

  • Latent Demand for Software: Engineering coding models have not replaced developers; instead, they have unlocked massive latent demand.
      • Higher Bar: Developers have never been able to do more, but society's expectations and the bar for quality have never been higher.
      • Human-Centric Obsession: Despite automation fears, public interest remains stubbornly focused on people, stories, and the human creators behind art and technology (e.g., the public reaction focusing on people involved rather than raw data center agent coordination).
  • Industrial Revolution vs. AI Renaissance: Altman contrasts two opposing future models for AI:
      • Industrial Revolution Model: Treating humans as specialized cognitive cogs in a machine.
      • Renaissance Model: A massive expansion of human potential, scientific discovery, art, and one-person company formation where people regain control of their time.

Protect Your Mornings: Sam Altman’s "Dot" Agent

  • Regaining Deep Work: Altman’s new always-on agent, "Dot" (or "Dottie"), acts as a persistent triage system that protects his early morning window of maximum creativity by handling overnight fires and surfacing only truly urgent matters.
  • Proactive Context Synthesis:
      • Cross-Platform Retrieval: Dot successfully scanned images and Slack screenshots across systems to find a lost reference after Altman spent 10–20 minutes failing to locate it using Codex search.
      • Live Iteration on Ideas: Altman uses voice notes on walks or right before sleep to dump raw feature ideas; Dot then builds 5 or 6 versions for him to review and iterate on during spare moments.
  • Live Collaboration Spaces: OpenAI built "Spaces" because traditional software abstractions break down when multiple people and multiple AI agents attempt to collaborate simultaneously on living documents and company dashboards that auto-update from Slack and real-world changes.

Platform Ecosystem & Empowering Startups

  • Platform Strategy: OpenAI explicitly rejects the philosophy that only one mega-corporation should exist; instead, they are betting heavily on an absolute lowering of the bar for one-person companies and small teams.
  • Bringing ChatGPT Subscriptions Everywhere: OpenAI's new developer tools allow users to bring their ChatGPT subscriptions directly to third-party app developers and plugin extensions, eliminating the financial penalty of letting users run heavy AI models.
  • Startup Golden Age: Altman notes that he has never seen startups get as successful as quickly, viewing the current ecosystem as an absolute golden age of entrepreneurship and creativity.

Inference, Speed, and "Ultrafast" Prompting

  • The Ultrafast Workflow: Altman conducts all of his prompting on "Ultrafast", arguing that immediate connection and rapid feedback loops fundamentally transform a creator's iterative thinking process.
  • The Price of IQ vs. Speed: OpenAI's historical focus has been driving down the price of intelligence; moving forward, speed and cost reduction will be pushed aggressively via custom chips and infrastructure investments to put high-speed inference into everyone's hands.

I think there are really two schools of thought among people building AI model companies. We want to go as hard as we can with the platform to enable people to do that. Although I think we haven't figured out the App Store yet, that's totally fair. I think we have figured out how to help millions of people build and create significant new products and companies. If you had to narrow in, you talked at the keynote about an industrial revolution versus a Renaissance, which you just mentioned now. If you had to narrow in on the key things that make the difference between those two worlds—and maybe OpenAI is part of it, but maybe even the broader ecosystem—what do you think those things are? One take on this would be that the Renaissance was fundamentally centered around people, and the industrial revolution was fundamentally centered around machines. There were a lot of good things about the industrial revolution. It had a hugely positive impact on the world in many ways, but I think a lot of people did not have a purely positive personal impact from that. This feeling that people had these rich lives and did all these things—yeah, of course they struggled and wanted more and better stuff and that's the part the industrial revolution did deliver in some ways—but they were kind of turned into, in many cases, this cognitive machine doing some extremely specialized thing and disconnected from a lot of the parts that made life good. I get sad to hear people talk about, "Oh, AI is going to automate everything, there's going to be no role for humans, we're going to be like house cats, and the AI will decide how to reward us and tell us what to do." That's certainly not what we think. What I am hopeful for is that this is just a huge new wave of scientific discovery, art, company formation—everybody that wants to start a new thing—and that people do get the benefits of all the economic growth and the new stuff happening here. But also, people get back in control of their lives and the ability to do the things they want and spend their time the way they want. One of the things that strikes me about the Renaissance versus the Industrial Revolution is that in the Renaissance you have a sort of different understanding of what it means to be a person and an individual as opposed to a cog in the machine. Do you feel like you're starting to see any difference in how we think of ourselves as humans that's comparable to the way that we changed how we think of ourselves during the Renaissance? I mean, so much happened during the Renaissance, it's hard to say there's this one thing. But definitely a big part of it was about people reconnecting to a lot of things that had been in the distant past, and then also this kind of combination of that with this crazy futurism and discovery of science and engineering, and just a feeling that human potential had massively expanded. You do hear people talk about both of those things simultaneously with AI, which is kind of how I got interested in this as an analogy in the first place. I mean, this is a very trivial example relative to what is going to be possible or already is possible, but the dots, the new thing we launched today, is the first thing that has given me back control of my mornings and my mental space in the mornings.
