We’re Reaching the Physical Limits of Chips

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(73.8K)·13:41·1.2K views·2026-09-30
Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Steven LatréVP of AI, imec Adam ChambersPartner, Strike Capital
TL;DRMemory prices have surged 700% this year amid severe physical limits in semiconductor manufacturing, driving an urgent hardware renaissance where power, cooling, and data transfer bottlenecks are reshaping the AI infrastructure stack.
Moore's Law Plateau: Steven Latré notes...

TL;DR

Memory prices have surged 700% this year amid severe physical limits in semiconductor manufacturing, driving an urgent hardware renaissance where power, cooling, and data transfer bottlenecks are reshaping the AI infrastructure stack.

Semiconductor Physics and Manufacturing Constraints

  • Moore's Law Plateau: Steven Latré notes that transistor scaling has reached physical limits, with depths now at roughly 1 nanometer (100,000 times smaller than a human hair), requiring significantly more inventive hardware approaches.
  • The Imec Role: Steven Latré describes imec as a 40-year-old Belgian R&D leader operating a unique clean room facility where almost every major chip in the world is designed 5 to 10 years ahead of commercialization.
  • Chip Design Diversification: Steven Latré states that endless market demand for custom silicon is driving the creation of new chip companies, requiring hardware diversification beyond standard GPUs to match emerging software algorithms.

Infrastructure Bottlenecks: Memory, Power, and Cooling

  • Memory Shortage & Price Spikes: Adam Chambers identifies memory bandwidth and latency as the primary constraint at the inference layer; Steven Latré notes memory is already "broken today."
      • Market Concentration: Adam Chambers points out that 95% of memory production is controlled by just three companies (Micron, SK Hynix, and Samsung), with two based in Korea.
      • Hyperscaler Impact: Adam Chambers notes that hyperscalers have absorbed entire inventory supplies, causing memory prices to rise by 700% this year.
  • Power and Cooling Challenges: Adam Chambers highlights power constraints as a massive bottleneck and predicts that liquid cooling will progress over the next 5 to 10 years by moving physically closer and closer to the GPU itself.

The Software-Hardware Paradigm Shift

  • Value Reversal: Adam Chambers references discussions with Tony Kim of BlackRock at the Raise AI Summit in Paris, noting a hardware renaissance where chip margins are fantastic while software faces compression.
  • Software Immaturity: Steven Latré argues the shift isn't a permanent flip of the pyramid, but rather reflects the immaturity of current AI software, which relies on a "brute force approach" that will eventually evolve to integrate more closely with hardware.
  • Model Size Limits: Steven Latré asserts that building ever-larger models will stop, noting that open-source models like K3D already exceed the human brain's parameters while remaining about a million times less energy efficient.

Photonics as the Next-Generation Interconnect

  • Overcoming Copper Limitations: Adam Chambers and Steven Latré agree that copper wiring has reached its limits due to latent heat and electrical inefficiencies.
  • Photonics Advantages: Adam Chambers explains that using light instead of electrons attacks multiple bottlenecks simultaneously by increasing transfer speeds, reducing cooling requirements, and improving core data transfer efficiency.
  • Evolution of Optical Architecture: Adam Chambers predicts that over the next 5 years, optical components will move closer to the GPU, shifting from linear pluggable optics between clusters to co-packaged optics.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Steven Latré predicts that in 5 to 10 years, people will laugh at today's AI and view large language models' brute-force approach the same way people now view dial-up internet connections.
  • Adam Chambers predicts that over the next 5 to 10 years, liquid cooling will progress by bringing cooling liquid closer and closer to the actual GPU.
  • Adam Chambers predicts that within the next 5 years, optical components will move closer to the GPU toward a state of co-packaged optics.
